But at the same time, I felt really tense, like I couldn’t just stop in the middle of it.\n\nThe bags had to be in that pattern, and the dispatch numbers had to be said the right way, or it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that doesn’t sound reasonable. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just… couldn’t step away right then, even when he told me to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these kinds of rituals or patterns outside of your job at the station?\n\nPatient: Um… outside the station, I don’t know if it’s really the same. The main thing was what happened there, with the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers. I know that probably sounds odd, but in the moment it felt like they had to be in the right pattern, and if I stopped before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I do get pulled into patterns, but that station thing is the one that got noticed. I wasn’t trying to make a scene or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to prevent something bad from happening when you arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I had some clear, logical reason, you know? It wasn’t like I thought, “this exact bad thing will happen.” It was more this feeling that if I didn’t put the airway bags in the pattern that felt right, and if I didn’t say the dispatch numbers the right way, then the whole bay would sort of… go bad.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m not trying to make excuses. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I wanted to listen, I really did, but it felt wrong to step away before the sequence was finished. Like I’d be leaving something unsafe or unfinished. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t think I wanted to be doing it, no. I mean… I wanted it to be done right, if that makes sense, but I wasn’t enjoying it or trying to be odd.

I know that sounds strange. I’m trying to explain it the right way. It felt more forced than chosen.

I guess… it feels alien, mostly. I don’t think of myself as someone who does things like that, if that makes sense.

I mean, they sent me here because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that I just knew was right, and I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. And when my lieutenant told me to stop, I should’ve stopped. I know that. I wasn’t trying to be difficult.

But in the moment, it felt like I couldn’t step away, because if I broke the sequence, it was like the whole bay would go bad somehow. That doesn’t feel like “me,” exactly… it feels like something that kind of takes over. Is that the kind of answer you’re asking for?

He told me to stop, but I couldn’t really step away right then. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How did you feel when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers?\n\nPatient: I felt… kind of embarrassed, honestly. Like I knew it probably looked bad, and I didn’t want to make trouble or ignore him. But at the same time, I felt really tense, like I couldn’t just stop in the middle of it.\n\nThe bags had to be in that pattern, and the dispatch numbers had to be said the right way, or it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that doesn’t sound reasonable. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just… couldn’t step away right then, even when he told me to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these kinds of rituals or patterns outside of your job at the station?\n\nPatient: Um… outside the station, I don’t know if it’s really the same. The main thing was what happened there, with the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers. I know that probably sounds odd, but in the moment it felt like they had to be in the right pattern, and if I stopped before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I do get pulled into patterns, but that station thing is the one that got noticed. I wasn’t trying to make a scene or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to prevent something bad from happening when you arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I had some clear, logical reason, you know? It wasn’t like I thought, “this exact bad thing will happen.” It was more this feeling that if I didn’t put the airway bags in the pattern that felt right, and if I didn’t say the dispatch numbers the right way, then the whole bay would sort of… go bad.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m not trying to make excuses. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I wanted to listen, I really did, but it felt wrong to step away before the sequence was finished. Like I’d be leaving something unsafe or unfinished. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like something you're forced to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I wanted to be doing it, no. I mean… I wanted it to be done right, if that makes sense, but I wasn’t enjoying it or trying to be odd.\n\nWhen they sent me here, it was because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I knew I should have just stopped. I didn’t want to make him upset or anything. But it felt like I couldn’t step away until the sequence was finished, like if I broke it, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m trying to explain it the right way. It felt more forced than chosen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels alien, mostly. I don’t think of myself as someone who does things like that, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, they sent me here because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that I just knew was right, and I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. And when my lieutenant told me to stop, I should’ve stopped. I know that. I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut in the moment, it felt like I couldn’t step away, because if I broke the sequence, it was like the whole bay would go bad somehow. That doesn’t feel like “me,” exactly… it feels like something that kind of takes over. Is that the kind of answer you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these patterns and rituals for a short period, or have they been going on for a while?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little while, I guess. Not my whole life or anything.\n\nIt really started after I hurt my shoulder and ribs during that ambulance transfer, about eight months ago. At first I thought it was just stress or me trying to keep things orderly, and I didn’t want to make a big deal out of it. But, yeah… if I’m answering you honestly, it’s been going on since around then. I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut in the moment, it felt like I couldn’t step away, because if I broke the sequence, it was like the whole bay would go bad somehow. That doesn’t feel like “me,” exactly… it feels like something that kind of takes over. Not my whole life or anything.\n\nIt really started after I hurt my shoulder and ribs during that ambulance transfer, about eight months ago. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions, is it because you're having a thought that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I think so… or, not like a normal thought exactly. It’s more like this pressure in my head that the pattern has to be finished the right way.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them until they fit the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I know that probably looked bad. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I wanted to listen, I really did, but I couldn’t step away because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not saying that makes sense. It’s just… that’s what it feels like when it’s happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… I don’t think I would choose it. I mean, I like things orderly, sure, but not like that.

At the station, moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud… that wasn’t me trying to be annoying or make a scene. I just felt like the pattern had to be right, and when my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should. I really did.

Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, sort of.

It’s… pretty high, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I can’t finish it, I get really unsettled. Like I can’t just shrug it off and move on.

I was just trying to get the bay set right.\n\nAt the station I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern that felt… correct, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud a few times. He told me to stop, but I couldn’t really step away right then. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How did you feel when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers?\n\nPatient: I felt… kind of embarrassed, honestly. Like I knew it probably looked bad, and I didn’t want to make trouble or ignore him. I know that doesn’t sound reasonable. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just… couldn’t step away right then, even when he told me to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these kinds of rituals or patterns outside of your job at the station?\n\nPatient: Um… outside the station, I don’t know if it’s really the same. The main thing was what happened there, with the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers. I know that probably sounds odd, but in the moment it felt like they had to be in the right pattern, and if I stopped before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I do get pulled into patterns, but that station thing is the one that got noticed. I wasn’t trying to make a scene or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to prevent something bad from happening when you arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair to say. It wasn’t like I thought, “this exact bad thing will happen.” It was more this feeling that if I didn’t put the airway bags in the pattern that felt right, and if I didn’t say the dispatch numbers the right way, then the whole bay would sort of… go bad.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m not trying to make excuses. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I wanted to listen, I really did, but it felt wrong to step away before the sequence was finished. Like I’d be leaving something unsafe or unfinished. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like something you're forced to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I wanted to be doing it, no. I mean… I wanted it to be done right, if that makes sense, but I wasn’t enjoying it or trying to be odd.\n\nWhen they sent me here, it was because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I knew I should have just stopped. I didn’t want to make him upset or anything. But it felt like I couldn’t step away until the sequence was finished, like if I broke it, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m trying to explain it the right way. But it felt like I couldn’t step away until the sequence was finished, like if I broke it, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m trying to explain it the right way. It felt more forced than chosen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels alien, mostly. I don’t think of myself as someone who does things like that, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, they sent me here because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that I just knew was right, and I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. And when my lieutenant told me to stop, I should’ve stopped. I know that. I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut in the moment, it felt like I couldn’t step away, because if I broke the sequence, it was like the whole bay would go bad somehow. That doesn’t feel like “me,” exactly… it feels like something that kind of takes over. Is that the kind of answer you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these patterns and rituals for a short period, or have they been going on for a while?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little while, I guess. Not my whole life or anything.\n\nIt really started after I hurt my shoulder and ribs during that ambulance transfer, about eight months ago. At first I thought it was just stress or me trying to keep things orderly, and I didn’t want to make a big deal out of it. But, yeah… if I’m answering you honestly, it’s been going on since around then. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions, is it because you're having a thought that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I think so… or, not like a normal thought exactly. It’s more like this pressure in my head that the pattern has to be finished the right way.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them until they fit the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I know that probably looked bad. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I wanted to listen, I really did, but I couldn’t step away because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not saying that makes sense. It’s just… that’s what it feels like when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these rituals are something that you would choose to do if you didn't feel compelled to do them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I would choose it. I mean, I like things orderly, sure, but not like that.\n\nAt the station, moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud… that wasn’t me trying to be annoying or make a scene. I just felt like the pattern had to be right, and when my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should. I really did.\n\nBut it felt like if I stepped away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. So no, I wouldn’t choose to do that if I didn’t feel pushed into it. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern, is it usually because a specific thought or image is repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, sort of.\n\nIt’s not always like a clear picture in my mind, like I’m imagining something on purpose. It’s more like the numbers and the order get stuck there, and I feel like I have to line things up until it matches. That’s what happened at the station with the airway bags… I kept moving them into the pattern that felt right and saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I wasn’t trying to ignore my lieutenant when he told me to stop. I just felt like if I stepped away before the sequence was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to complete one of these rituals or patterns?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty high, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I can’t finish it, I get really unsettled. Like I can’t just shrug it off and move on.\n\nAt the station, that’s kind of what happened. I kept trying to get the airway bags into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should’ve just stopped. I didn’t want to cause trouble.\n\nBut it felt like if I stepped away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. So… yeah, it’s more distressing than I want to admit. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these rituals and patterns affecting your ability to do your job or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a small problem, but it has been affecting things.\n\nI can’t really work the ambulance anymore. And even when they tried putting me on desk-duty stuff—notes, equipment checks—I wasn’t keeping up with it reliably enough. I guess I kept getting stuck, and they ended up taking me off the schedule.\n\nAt home, I’m not doing as much as I used to either. And I’ve kind of stopped seeing friends for the most part. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just giving up or anything… but yeah, it’s gotten in the way a lot. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I try not to focus on them, but the numbers or the order of things kind of keeps coming back.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags… I kept moving them into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I knew it probably looked bad, and I knew my lieutenant wanted me to stop, but it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away too soon, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that doesn’t sound very reasonable. I’m not trying to be difficult. It just gets stuck in my head, and ignoring it feels… really hard. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How did you feel when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers?\n\nPatient: I felt… kind of embarrassed, honestly. Like I knew it probably looked bad, and I didn’t want to make trouble or ignore him. But at the same time, I felt really tense, like I couldn’t just stop in the middle of it.\n\nThe bags had to be in that pattern, and the dispatch numbers had to be said the right way, or it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that doesn’t sound reasonable. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just… couldn’t step away right then, even when he told me to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these kinds of rituals or patterns outside of your job at the station?\n\nPatient: Um… outside the station, I don’t know if it’s really the same. The main thing was what happened there, with the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers. I know that probably sounds odd, but in the moment it felt like they had to be in the right pattern, and if I stopped before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I do get pulled into patterns, but that station thing is the one that got noticed. I wasn’t trying to make a scene or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to prevent something bad from happening when you arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I had some clear, logical reason, you know? It wasn’t like I thought, “this exact bad thing will happen.” It was more this feeling that if I didn’t put the airway bags in the pattern that felt right, and if I didn’t say the dispatch numbers the right way, then the whole bay would sort of… go bad.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m not trying to make excuses. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I wanted to listen, I really did, but it felt wrong to step away before the sequence was finished. Like I’d be leaving something unsafe or unfinished. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like something you're forced to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I wanted to be doing it, no. I mean… I wanted it to be done right, if that makes sense, but I wasn’t enjoying it or trying to be odd.\n\nWhen they sent me here, it was because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I knew I should have just stopped. I didn’t want to make him upset or anything. But it felt like I couldn’t step away until the sequence was finished, like if I broke it, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m trying to explain it the right way. It felt more forced than chosen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels alien, mostly. I don’t think of myself as someone who does things like that, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, they sent me here because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that I just knew was right, and I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. And when my lieutenant told me to stop, I should’ve stopped. I know that. I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut in the moment, it felt like I couldn’t step away, because if I broke the sequence, it was like the whole bay would go bad somehow. That doesn’t feel like “me,” exactly… it feels like something that kind of takes over. Is that the kind of answer you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these patterns and rituals for a short period, or have they been going on for a while?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little while, I guess. Not my whole life or anything.\n\nIt really started after I hurt my shoulder and ribs during that ambulance transfer, about eight months ago. At first I thought it was just stress or me trying to keep things orderly, and I didn’t want to make a big deal out of it. But, yeah… if I’m answering you honestly, it’s been going on since around then. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions, is it because you're having a thought that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I think so… or, not like a normal thought exactly. It’s more like this pressure in my head that the pattern has to be finished the right way.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them until they fit the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I know that probably looked bad. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I wanted to listen, I really did, but I couldn’t step away because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not saying that makes sense. It’s just… that’s what it feels like when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these rituals are something that you would choose to do if you didn't feel compelled to do them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I would choose it. I mean, I like things orderly, sure, but not like that.\n\nAt the station, moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud… that wasn’t me trying to be annoying or make a scene. I just felt like the pattern had to be right, and when my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should. I really did.\n\nBut it felt like if I stepped away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. So no, I wouldn’t choose to do that if I didn’t feel pushed into it. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern, is it usually because a specific thought or image is repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, sort of.\n\nIt’s not always like a clear picture in my mind, like I’m imagining something on purpose. It’s more like the numbers and the order get stuck there, and I feel like I have to line things up until it matches. That’s what happened at the station with the airway bags… I kept moving them into the pattern that felt right and saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I wasn’t trying to ignore my lieutenant when he told me to stop. I just felt like if I stepped away before the sequence was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to complete one of these rituals or patterns?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty high, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I can’t finish it, I get really unsettled. Like I can’t just shrug it off and move on.\n\nAt the station, that’s kind of what happened. I kept trying to get the airway bags into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should’ve just stopped. I didn’t want to cause trouble.\n\nBut it felt like if I stepped away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. So… yeah, it’s more distressing than I want to admit. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these rituals and patterns affecting your ability to do your job or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a small problem, but it has been affecting things.\n\nI can’t really work the ambulance anymore. And even when they tried putting me on desk-duty stuff—notes, equipment checks—I wasn’t keeping up with it reliably enough. I guess I kept getting stuck, and they ended up taking me off the schedule.\n\nAt home, I’m not doing as much as I used to either. And I’ve kind of stopped seeing friends for the most part. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just giving up or anything… but yeah, it’s gotten in the way a lot. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I try not to focus on them, but the numbers or the order of things kind of keeps coming back.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags… I kept moving them into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I knew it probably looked bad, and I knew my lieutenant wanted me to stop, but it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away too soon, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that doesn’t sound very reasonable. I’m not trying to be difficult. It just gets stuck in my head, and ignoring it feels… really hard. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern, is it always in response to a thought that is repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, I don’t think… or maybe I don’t notice the thought first every time.\n\nAt the station, though, it was like that. I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I knew it probably wasn’t helping anything, and I know my lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn’t really make myself step away because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not trying to make excuses. That’s just what it felt like in the moment. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are the patterns you feel compelled to follow usually triggered by a specific thought or mental image that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Usually, yeah… or at least that’s how it feels when it gets bad.\n\nLike, at the station, it wasn’t just me wanting things neat. I kept seeing how the airway bags needed to be lined up, like there was a right pattern, and I kept repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. I knew my lieutenant wanted me to stop, and I know it probably looked… not great. But I couldn’t step away, because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not saying that makes sense. I just—at the time it feels really hard to let it go. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to complete these rituals, are you worried that something bad will happen to you or to someone else if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I was worried something bad would happen, but not exactly like I could explain it clearly.\n\nAt the station, when I was moving the airway bags into that pattern and saying the dispatch numbers, it felt like if I stopped or broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that sounds strange. I wasn’t trying to ignore my lieutenant or cause a problem… I just couldn’t step away right then.\n\nI don’t know if I thought it would hurt a specific person, exactly. It was more like this heavy feeling that something would go wrong if I didn’t finish it the right way. Is that what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s what it felt like. Not in a way I could explain logically, but at the station, when I was moving the airway bags into that pattern and repeating the dispatch numbers, it felt like if I didn’t finish it right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.

I know that probably sounds extreme. I wasn’t trying to be difficult with my lieutenant when he told me to stop… I just couldn’t make myself step away right then. Does that make sense?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess my lieutenant thought I should come in. It wasn’t anything dangerous, I don’t think. I was just trying to get the bay set right.\n\nAt the station I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern that felt… correct, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud a few times. He told me to stop, but I couldn’t really step away right then. It felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that probably sounds strange.\n\nDoctor: How did you feel when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers?\n\nPatient: I felt… kind of embarrassed, honestly. Like I knew it probably looked bad, and I didn’t want to make trouble or ignore him. But at the same time, I felt really tense, like I couldn’t just stop in the middle of it.\n\nThe bags had to be in that pattern, and the dispatch numbers had to be said the right way, or it felt like the whole bay would go bad somehow. I know that doesn’t sound reasonable. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just… couldn’t step away right then, even when he told me to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to perform these kinds of rituals or patterns outside of your job at the station?\n\nPatient: Um… outside the station, I don’t know if it’s really the same. The main thing was what happened there, with the airway bags and saying the dispatch numbers. I know that probably sounds odd, but in the moment it felt like they had to be in the right pattern, and if I stopped before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… I guess I do get pulled into patterns, but that station thing is the one that got noticed. I wasn’t trying to make a scene or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to prevent something bad from happening when you arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I had some clear, logical reason, you know? It wasn’t like I thought, “this exact bad thing will happen.” It was more this feeling that if I didn’t put the airway bags in the pattern that felt right, and if I didn’t say the dispatch numbers the right way, then the whole bay would sort of… go bad.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m not trying to make excuses. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I wanted to listen, I really did, but it felt wrong to step away before the sequence was finished. Like I’d be leaving something unsafe or unfinished. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like something you're forced to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I wanted to be doing it, no. I mean… I wanted it to be done right, if that makes sense, but I wasn’t enjoying it or trying to be odd.\n\nWhen they sent me here, it was because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I knew I should have just stopped. I didn’t want to make him upset or anything. But it felt like I couldn’t step away until the sequence was finished, like if I broke it, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I’m trying to explain it the right way. It felt more forced than chosen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and rituals are a part of who you are, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels alien, mostly. I don’t think of myself as someone who does things like that, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, they sent me here because at the station I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern that I just knew was right, and I was repeating the dispatch numbers out loud. And when my lieutenant told me to stop, I should’ve stopped. I know that. I wasn’t trying to be difficult.\n\nBut in the moment, it felt like I couldn’t step away, because if I broke the sequence, it was like the whole bay would go bad somehow. That doesn’t feel like “me,” exactly… it feels like something that kind of takes over. Is that the kind of answer you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these patterns and rituals for a short period, or have they been going on for a while?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little while, I guess. Not my whole life or anything.\n\nIt really started after I hurt my shoulder and ribs during that ambulance transfer, about eight months ago. At first I thought it was just stress or me trying to keep things orderly, and I didn’t want to make a big deal out of it. But, yeah… if I’m answering you honestly, it’s been going on since around then. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern or repeat certain actions, is it because you're having a thought that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I think so… or, not like a normal thought exactly. It’s more like this pressure in my head that the pattern has to be finished the right way.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them until they fit the pattern I knew was right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I know that probably looked bad. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I wanted to listen, I really did, but I couldn’t step away because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not saying that makes sense. It’s just… that’s what it feels like when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these rituals are something that you would choose to do if you didn't feel compelled to do them?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I would choose it. I mean, I like things orderly, sure, but not like that.\n\nAt the station, moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud… that wasn’t me trying to be annoying or make a scene. I just felt like the pattern had to be right, and when my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should. I really did.\n\nBut it felt like if I stepped away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. So no, I wouldn’t choose to do that if I didn’t feel pushed into it. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern, is it usually because a specific thought or image is repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s tied to that, sort of.\n\nIt’s not always like a clear picture in my mind, like I’m imagining something on purpose. It’s more like the numbers and the order get stuck there, and I feel like I have to line things up until it matches. That’s what happened at the station with the airway bags… I kept moving them into the pattern that felt right and saying the dispatch numbers out loud.\n\nI know that sounds strange. I wasn’t trying to ignore my lieutenant when he told me to stop. I just felt like if I stepped away before the sequence was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you're unable to complete one of these rituals or patterns?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty high, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I can’t finish it, I get really unsettled. Like I can’t just shrug it off and move on.\n\nAt the station, that’s kind of what happened. I kept trying to get the airway bags into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. When my lieutenant told me to stop, I knew I should’ve just stopped. I didn’t want to cause trouble.\n\nBut it felt like if I stepped away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow. So… yeah, it’s more distressing than I want to admit. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these rituals and patterns affecting your ability to do your job or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a small problem, but it has been affecting things.\n\nI can’t really work the ambulance anymore. And even when they tried putting me on desk-duty stuff—notes, equipment checks—I wasn’t keeping up with it reliably enough. I guess I kept getting stuck, and they ended up taking me off the schedule.\n\nAt home, I’m not doing as much as I used to either. And I’ve kind of stopped seeing friends for the most part. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just giving up or anything… but yeah, it’s gotten in the way a lot. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I try not to focus on them, but the numbers or the order of things kind of keeps coming back.\n\nLike at the station, with the airway bags… I kept moving them into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I knew it probably looked bad, and I knew my lieutenant wanted me to stop, but it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away too soon, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI know that doesn’t sound very reasonable. I’m not trying to be difficult. It just gets stuck in my head, and ignoring it feels… really hard. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to arrange things in a specific pattern, is it always in response to a thought that is repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, I don’t think… or maybe I don’t notice the thought first every time.\n\nAt the station, though, it was like that. I kept moving the airway bags into the pattern that felt right, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud. I knew it probably wasn’t helping anything, and I know my lieutenant told me to stop, but I couldn’t really make myself step away because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nI’m not trying to make excuses. That’s just what it felt like in the moment. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are the patterns you feel compelled to follow usually triggered by a specific thought or mental image that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Usually, yeah… or at least that’s how it feels when it gets bad.\n\nLike, at the station, it wasn’t just me wanting things neat.
